[trialAccount t18420822-2538]
2538. DENNIS CLEMENTS was indicted for the wilful murder of Bridget Holloway.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
SOPHIA VINCENT . I am the wife of Benjamin Vincent, of Smith's-rents, East-lane, Greenwich—I have lived there going on for four years—I knew Bridget Holloway, the deceased—she lived in the next room to me—mine is the front, hers was the back—a partition of wood separates our rooms—you can hear what passes from one room to the other—you can hear persons talk—the prisoner was in the habit of going to the room in which the deceased lived—I thought, at first, they were man and wife—he is a pensioner in the Hospital—on Sunday, the 10th of July, about nine o'clock at night, I was in my own room, and heard the cry of "Murder" coming from the deceased's room several times—I did not hear any other person but her—she continued to cry "Murder" several times, until the cries grew fainter—I went down and told Mrs. Smith, who was at the door—I then went back to my room, and heard no more noise, or cries that night—next day I went into Bridget Holloway's room, about half-past twelve—I brought her up some hot water—I found her sitting on her bed undressed, in her petticoat and shift—she told me she was very bad—she could hardly speak—she was obliged to stop two or three times between the words—she showed me her left side—it was all bruised black and discoloured—she said she should never get over it, for she was sure her ribs were broken—she appeared to be labouring under the impression that she should die—she said the prisoner had done it by constantly knocking her on the side—I saw her again on Tuesday evening, and then no more till after her death—she died on Thursday—about twelve o'clock on Tuesday night I was in my own room, and heard her groans—I never heard her any
more—she told me, on Tuesday, that she was a great deal worse, and she complained of her left side, that she could not lie—it was the same place she had pointed out to me before—on the Thursday after her death, I saw the prisoner at Dr. Mitchell's, and asked him if she had been worse the night before—he said no, she had been better—I said she could not be better, for she complained of being very bad—he then began to abuse me—I told him he had ill-used her—he denied it—I did not say anything about the cries I had heard, because Mr. Mitchell came in, and it was stopped—he went out.
Prisoner. Q. Did you ever see me drunk, or ill-use her? A. I have heard you ill-use her several times before—I was never in her room when you and she were there, but I have heard you ill-use her many a time with your tongue—I never saw you strike her.
SUSAN MARY MATTHEWS . I lived in this house. On Sunday night, the 10th of July, about nine o'clock, I saw the prisoner come out of the deceased's room—she called him an infernal rogue, and said he had done for her now—he had given her a death-blow—as he passed by the door. he made answer, "You infernal b—h, it serves you right."
Prisoner. Q. Did you hear me say so? A. Yes, I did—I saw you go down stairs—my door was open.
Prisoner. God forgive you! I made no answer.
SUSAN MART SMITH . I am the wife of Henry Smith, a shoemaker, and live in Smith's-rents, in the room under Bridget Holloway. On Sunday evening, the 10th of July, about eight o'clock, I was going down stairs, and met her on the stairs—she appeared to be as well as usual—the spoke to me—I do not know what age she was—she appeared about seventy—Mrs. Vincent came and told me something; that evening, in consequence of which, I went up into my own room, and heard the deceased crying bitterly, in her own room—I did not see her any more that night—about half-past eight in the morning, I heard her knocking in her room—I went out to see what it was, and then went up into her room—she was just sat down on the bed—she was alone—when she spoke she put her hand to her left side, and it was several seconds before she could speak—she showed me her bruises—I observed bruises on her side—she said she was sure her ribs were broken, and he had given her her death-blow—she had a bruise on each arm, a bruise on her breast, and a slight one on the leg, besides those on the side—I went up about one o'clock, and took her a little broth, thinking it might do her good—she seemed in more agonizing pain than before—she was in the habit of going into the streets with a basket, for the last eight months—she went out that afternoon, between three and four o'clock—I carried her basket down stairs for her—she seemed very bad, and still complained of being very ill, that he had given her her deathblow, and she should never get over it—she returned between six and seven—I saw her next day, Tuesday, between three and four in the afternoon—she appeared to be getting worse—I did not see her on Wednesday—I heard her in her room about half-past twelve at night, groaning very much indeed—on Thursday morning, about half-past seven, the prisoner opened my door, came in, and said, "She is gone; she is gone"—another person said directly, "Is she gone to the Union?"—he said, "No; she is dead, she is dead"—in consequence of that, I went up stairs into the room, and found her dead, but not quite cold—there was a bruise to be seen then on her side as she was lying—the prisoner said, "What do you think will be done in this case?"—I
said I did not know, it would appear very dark against him—he said, "The bruise was done by her daughter, some years ago"—I said "It would have been worn out before this time"—he made no answer to that—we all three then left the room.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me come down stairs drunk while there, or abuse the woman, or kick up any noise all the time you had been there? A. No—I was never up in the room—I have heard that you ill-treated her very much before I went there—I never saw or heard you ill-use her—I never heard any quarrelling.
THOMAS OAK MITCHELL . I am a surgeon, and live at Greenwich. I knew the deceased before this occurred—I was called to see her after her death—the prisoner applied to me to give a certificate of the cause of death—I had attended the woman twelve months prior, for a rheumatic affection—at the time the prisoner called on me he stated that she had been in a very infirm state of health a long time—I left the room, to consult a medical report book—on my return, I found Mrs. Vincent there, and in consequence of hearing the word "ill-treatment" made use of, I refused the certificate—I afterwards, by the order of the Coroner, examined the body externally—it presented appearances of bruises, extending from the left shoulder, over the left side, nearly to the pelvis, and also marks of bruises on the right and left arm—four ribs were fractured, two of which were found penetrating a portion of the left lung, which, in my judgment, was the immediate cause of death—the most prominent symptom was the difficulty of breathing—a blow or a kick would most probably produce such an injury—it must have been very violent—I should say she had been suffering from the injury three or four days.
Prisoner. She had been under him once before with a very bad cough, and was ailing; she took liquor, and has fallen down stairs; it is not the first time I have taken her up stairs, bruised, and complaining of her side. Witness. I never attended her before with any complaint of that description—she was subject to rheumatic affection in the winter—the body was in other respects perfectly healthy—the fracture of the ribs was the cause of death, by producing inflammation of the lungs—I doubt whether, if it had been seen to in the first instance, that life could have been saved, from the position in which the ribs were, and the lung being lacerated—it might have given her a chance.
JOSEPH BERRIDGE . I am a surgeon. I assisted Mr. Mitchell in the post mortem examination—he has correctly stated the appearances of the body—I agree with him entirely in his opinion as to the cause of death.
JEREMIAH COOLAN . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner into custody on this charge, and told him I took him on suspicion of killing a woman—he said he was innocent—in taking him to the station, I asked him How the wounds were inflicted on the body—he said they were caused by falling down stairs.
Prisoner's Defence. Some friends from London came to see me, and after dinner we went to a public-house; the deceased drank with us all the afternoon; I went to see my friends off, and then went home; the old woman was pretty well, only had drank too much; I was sitting in my chair, when she took a start, and said, "I want some money;" I looked at her, and said, "I have got none to give you, you have had plenty to drink. without having any more;" she said, "You b—thief, if you don't give it to me I will knock you down;" she took up the hair-broom to give me
a knock on the head; she said, "I don't expect to live long, and I will leave you in trouble before I go;" I took the broom out of her hand, put it in the corner, and was going out, when she caught hold of my collar, dragged me back, and said, "You want to go out to your wh—s, you must give roe some money;" I said, "Let go;" she would not, but was dragging and striking me; I gave her a shove with one band, and she went back right against the bedstead; she screamed, "Murder!" I said, "Oh, you villain, this is what you wanted, to bring me into trouble;" I went out, and went to the College; I saw her next day, about two o'clock, sitting in the street with her basket, but never spoke to her, and on Tuesday morning I saw her in the same place; she shook her head and hand, and said, "You b—thief, I will fetch you in before I go, I know my time is not long;" she went up stairs, and breathed hard; she was a very unhealthy woman; I saw her again that night come out of a public-house; I paid her rent, but never lived with her, only for shame's take; the followed me from London; I gave her up several times, but she would come after me; I saw her several times on Wednesday; she seemed as well as ever, and I bought her sixpenny worth of cakes to sell next day; when I left her at night she told me to lock the door, and take the key with me, which I did, and went to the College; when I came, about seven o'clock next morning, she was in bed; that frightened roe, and I ran down and told the people that she was gone; they went up, and she had her hands clenched, as if she was in convulsions; I went and told Mr. Mitchell of it, he told me the Jury must sit on her, and I had her laid out.
GUILTY of Manslaughter. Aged 68.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined One Year.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18420822-2539]
2539. SARAH WESTCOTT was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Richard Killick, about twelve o'clock in the night of the 30th of July, at St. Paul, Deptford, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 1 pair of boots, value 2 s., his property.
RICHARD KILLICK . I am a shoemaker, and live in the Broadway, in the parish of St. Paul, Deptford—I keep the house. On the 30th of July, about twenty minutes before twelve o'clock at night, I saw the prisoner at my window—she put her hand under her shawl, and deliberately hit a pane of glass, but did not break it—she then hit it again, broke it, and drew the boots through the window—I followed her out, and she dropped them just before I got hold of her—I saw one fall from her.
Prisoner. It was his wife laid hold of me; I slipped against the window and broke it, but did not take the boots; he did not come out for five minutes. Witness. She took them, and they were thrown down at a distance from the window—my wife was going up stairs to bed at the time—I am quite sure it was a wilful act—the boots were picked up about five yards from the window—the prisoner has no mother, and I rather think she is not properly looked to.
JOHN WHITE . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner—I asked How she came to do it—she said she did not know—I took hold of her—she said, "Don't take hold of me, I will go with pleasure."
Prisoner. He said I was very foolish not to take a new pair, and asked
whether my father sent me to do it. Witness. I did not—they are secondhand.
Evidence for the Prisoner.
I am the prisoner's father and am a hair-dresser. I had sent her out on this Saturday night for half-an-ounce of pepper—she would only have to go round the corner, and not to pass the prosecutor's house—she has left me once or twice since Easter.
GUILTY. Aged 14.— Recommended to mercy.— Judgment Respited.
[trialAccount t18420822-2540]
2540. PATRICK BURNS was indicted for unlawfully, maliciously, and feloniously assaulting Michael Thomas Cannon, on the 11th of August, and cutting and wounding him in and upon the head and left hand, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
MICHAEL THOMAS CANNON . I am a policeman. On the 11th of August, about four o'clock in the morning, I was on duty in Woolwich Road, and noticed the prisoner coming from Mr. Miles's garden—I followed him and asked what he did on the premises—he said he was on a spree all night—I told him to come off the premises, up the lane leading towards the road—he complained of thirst—I said he could get some water from the pump—he had no cap or stock on—he carried a stick in his right hand and a stone in his left—he is a soldier—I suspected he had been stealing the fruit, and told him to give the stick up, making an effort to take it from him, he up with the stick and struck me over the right shoulder, and again over the right temple—he said he thought I was one of the picquet, and was there to take him into custody—he struck me several times—I took my staff out, and then we exchanged several blows—we both fell on the ground—there was a piece of iron, about five inches long, at the end of the stick he struck me with—it is termed a chevaux-de-frise —he afterwards drove this part of the stick into my hand—I did not find any fruit upon him—he never gave any account why he was in the garden.
Prisoner. Q. Which end of the stick did I hit you with? A. With both—I have suffered in my hand and on my head ever since—I did not strike you till you had struck me four or five blows—I did not seize you by the breast before you struck me—you came from an enclosed garden or orchard, with fruit trees and vegetables in it.
GEORGE MATTISON . I am a labouring man. On the morning of the 11th of August, I was going towards Woolwich and saw the prisoner and prosecutor struggling in the road—I saw the prisoner give the policeman some heavy blows—the prisoner sprung up and tried to get the stave from his hand, and threw him down—the prisoner came again, and struck him on the head with the iron end of the stick—the prisoner was secured.
Prisoner. Q. How far off were you while I was engaged with him? A. Close to you at the last of it—I saw him knock the stick from your hand—he did not knock you down, he threw you down at last—you did not say to me, "Is a soldier to be murdered on the road?"—you said, "Civilian, I am killed" —I said, "Go quietly, I cannot help it."
JOHN TESTER ( police-constable R 89.) On the morning of the 11th of August, I was on duty in Woolwich Road, the adjoining beat to the prosecutor's—I saw him and the prisoner struggling on the road, and both bleeding—I took up the chevaux-de-frise —when I came up, he said to the prosecutor, "You b—, if I had known you were coming to assist him,
I would have murdered you when I had you down, I would have kicked you on the head;" and on his way to the station he said he would lay wait for him when he got at liberty, and would put an end to him some day, when he could make it convenient.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me lying down or sitting on the road? A. You went and sat down as I came up to you.
EDWARD DOWLING . I am surgeon to the police force at Greenwich. On the morning of the 11th of August, I examined the constable Cannon—he had a contused wound on the left side of the head, two on the right side, a contusion on the right temple, a wound on the hand, and other marks of violence on his body—I considered the wound on the hand very serious, there was a great deal of inflammation about the arm, it had wounded the ligaments, and appeared to be done with great force—the prisoner had a severe contused laceration, such as a policeman's staff would make—the policeman's wounds were such as the iron end of the instrument would make—I think his head and hands were struck with that end.
Prisoner's Defence. Coming out in the morning about four o'clock, over the gate of the place, the constable came up and said, "Where are you going?" I said, "What is that to you?" be said, "You must give an account of yourself; I said I was on a bit of a spree all night; he said, "Well, I have been an old soldier myself, and have had many a spree;" I laid hold of the handle of the pump; he laid hold of my hand, and said, "You must give up the stick; I said, "Why?" he said, "Come with me to the station;" I would not; he then up with his staff, and struck me; I said, "What would it be for a soldier to come along with a policeman?" and he then struck me before I struck him; he tore the breast of my shirt off; I said, "I am a-soldier, and will go to my barrack?;" he then struck me down; his wounds are not so much as they appear with that plaster over them; I picked the instrument up in the road.
MICHAEL THOMAS CANNON re-examined. I did not lay hold of him at all till he struck me with the stick—this instrument is used at Woolwich to fence in the cannon.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Three Years.
Before Mr. Baron Rolfe.
[trialAccount t18420822-2541]
2541. JOSEPH HUGHES was indicted for feloniously uttering a forged receipt for 12 l., knowing it to be forged, with intent to defraud Samuel Burrows.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
MARGARET BURROWS . I am the wife of Samuel Burrows, who is now at sea. On the 8th of February I took a coffee-house of the prisoner, called The Arundel, at Greenwich—I agreed to pay 35 l. a year—he was to be my lodger, and to occupy one bed-room, at 3 s. a week, and to pay extra for what meals he had there—on the 3rd of May he made out this account, and delivered it to me—I think it she as 5 l. 3 s. 10 d. owing to me—on the 8th of May a quarter's rent would become due, which was 8 l. 15 s., and on the 4th of July, a person was put into possession for it—on the 9th of July I signed an agreement to let it remain for three weeks—I have not got that agreement, I believe one of the condemning brokers has it—I do not know whether he is here—on the 3rd of August my demand against the prisoner was 14 l. 5 s. 3 d.—on the 6th of August I
still owed him 8 l. 15 s. for a quarter's rent—there would be another quarter die pm the 8th pf August—on Saturday, the 6th of August, I met the prisoner at the office of Mr. Smith, a solicitor, at Green which, by appointment, for the purpose of settling the accounts between us, and whatever rent was due to him, I was willing and prepared to pay him by the Monday—14 l. 15 s. 3 d. was due to me, and two quarters' rent to him—I should have had to pay him 2 l. 14 s. 9 d.—when I met him at Mr. Smith's he produced this receipt for 12 l., purporting to bear my signature—I had never signed it, nor ever saw it or knew of it till then—I never borrowed 12 l. of the prisoner, nor ever had a fraction in the shape of money from him—he said it was for 12 l. which he had paid me—a man named Tuck was produced for the prisoner before the Magistrate, as a witness to the money being paid—I think he was sworn.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. The prisoner was obliged to put in a distress on the 3rd of July? A. Because he did not pay his accounts—he put a distress in, and took it off on my promising to pay—I did not pay at that time—I have not had a great many disagreements with people about payments—I cannot tell How many—I do not know whether Mr. Smith is here—I have not brought him here—the first broker employer when this distress was put in was Mr. Parker—I did not see Mr. Dunford on that occasion—since the second distress I did—at that time the prisoner owed me nearly 15 l.—I have the account here—I told Mr. Dunford that—that I swear—the second distress was made on the 9th of August—Mr. Dunford was there when the first distress was made—he was one of the condemning brokers—Parker was the distraining broker—I did not see Mr. Dunford on the second occasion, nor on any other occasion than the one I have spoken of—a man named Maberley was the broker on the second occasion—I never charged other people with forging receipts—that I swear—I know Mary Andrews and her husband signed it or not I do not know, and therefore I could not charge him with it—I never threatened to charge him with forging a receipt—that I swear—I never threatened to charge him with forging my name to a receipt of 3 l. at the Custom-house—there was a book produced which was receipted, but I never charged him with doing it—I never charged or threatened to charge him with forging my name—I never gave him a receipt in pencil for that 3 l.—I cannot tell How often I have been at the Police-office at Greenwich—it might be often—I have gone when I have had occasion to go—I cannot tell you How often—I cannot tell whether I have been there twenty times, thirty times, or forty times—I do not know at all—I cannot tell whether I have not been forty times before the Justice, making charges against individuals—I will not swear that I have not made forty charges against forty different individuals there—I charged a man once with not paying for refreshments which he had at my house—he said he had paid me, but he had not—I charged him with an assault, but it originated in my not being paid—I was on that occasion charged with kicking a man in a certain place—I was not fined—I had to pay 10 s.—I do not know whether I was fined, or whether it was the expenses—I preferred an indictment against a man for perjury, after being before the Justice—the bill was not thrown out—I did not come into the witness box—the man was never tried—I never said to Mr. Dunford since I preferred this charge, that for 10 l. I would squash this indictment, nor that I would make a
flaw in it—I never said, "If Hughes will give up the note, and pay me the difference I won't hurt him"—nor if I had 10 l. from him I would go into the country and squash the concern—I never said so to any body—I did not say, "You know, Mr. Dunford, a word before the Grand Jury will do it"—I did not after the prisoner was in charge go to Sergeant Good and tell him I suspected he had committed a murder—I did not tell him there was a smell in the room, and I believed a murder had been committed there—I did not say so to Arthur—I said, there was a very offensive smell in the room, something I could not possibly make out, and requested their interference—I said it was certainly like a dead body, or something of that kind—it was a very unnatural smell—I never said to Good, or Arthur that I believed there was a murdered man in the prisoner's room—I remember seeing Good and Arthur when I was in company with the witness Weale—Arthur did not on that occasion tax me with having taken a false oath before the Magistrate—I did not say I had done so to save myself the trouble I had got into, that I had got into trouble enough about this business already, and I did not want any more—I never alluded to a false oath—nothing of the sort occurred.
MR. PATNE. Q. Have you ever on any occasion, when you have gone before a Magistrate, made a false or incorrect charge? A. Not that I know of—I have always strictly stated the truth—I have not kept an account of the number of times, I have had occasion to take people before a Magistrate, or to go before a Magistrate—I should say I have not been forty times before a Magistrate.
JOHN HEALEY . I am a solicitor, and live in Keppel-street, Blackfriars-road—I have known Mrs. Burrows rather more than eleven years, and am very well acquainted with her hand-writing—I have seen this receipt before, at Mr. Smith's office, and before the Magistrate—the words " Mergret Burrows," and the name above that, is certainly not in her handwriting—the body of it the prisoner says be wrote himself—the name if Dot at all like her writing—she cannot write so well—I never saw any so good.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Why, is that not very bad? A. It is not good—I have seen a good deal worse—I am not practising as an attorney for myself, and have not for the last sixteen years—I have not taken out my certificate—I have been acting at clerk in other offices, but I am a solicitor—I last saw Mrs. Burrows write about a week or ten days ago, and I had seen her write a day or two before the charge was made before the Magistrate—she has consulted me on every occasion where, she has been in trouble since her husband went away—in the early part of the time I was acquainted with her she consulted me as regarded her property, previous to her marriage, and subsequently she has consulted me very often indeed, with regard to this business at Greenwich, I mean the business between her and the prisoner—not about this note—I was not her attorney in the assault cases—I never went to the police-court at Greenwich on her account—she consulted me upon that, and I advised her to go to Mr. May, and take him with her—I have never been there with her—I am not the attorney for this prosecution.
ELIZA WEALE . I have lived for several months with Mrs. Burrows, assisting her in her business at Greenwich, since last Easter. On the 4th of July, a distress was put into the house for rent, and a second distress was put in this month—on the first occasion I heard her say to the prisoner "It
is very hard that you should distrain on me for rent, knowing that you owe me more for board and lodging"—he said, "I will have my rent, you may get what I owe you in the best way you can"—I never heard him mention a sum of 12 l. that he had lent or paid her, or anything of the kind.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Were they not quarrelling? A. Yes—it was on the Saturday before the first distress—I heard Mrs. Burrows ask the prisoner to come to terms on the Monday that the distraint was put in, and several times.
MR. PAYNE. Q. The conversation took place on the Monday, and the quarrel on the Saturday? A. Yes.
GEORGE GOOD ( police-sergeant R 2.) The prisoner was given into my custody by Mrs. Burrows, at the coffee-shop, on Tuesday, the 9th of August, for forging a receipt—I asked him to produce the receipt, that I might show it to her—he refused to do so—he went up stairs—I followed him, and Mrs. Burrows came immediately after me—I did not see her till I got into the room—I went in after the prisoner—I asked him to let me see the receipt, so that Mrs. Burrows might see it—he said he was advised not to show it, and would not show it any more—I had seen it before—he afterwards pulled it out, and laid it on the table—while it was on the table, Mrs. Burrows came in—she said that it was a forgery, it was not hers, she had never seen it before till she had seen it at Mr. Smith's office—the prisoner took it off the table and put it into his pocket—I took him into custody, found the receipt on him, took possession of it, and have produced it to-day—the prisoner called a man named Tuck, as a witness, before the Magistrate.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. He did not object to show you the receipt? A. He did at first—he would not show it to me before Mrs. Burrows, but I had seen it before—he showed it to me, with the broker, when Mrs. Burrows was not present—I remember being in company with Mrs. Burrows and the witness Weale, in the presence of Arthur, a police-constable, a day or two after I had taken the prisoner—we were in a room in Mrs. Burrows' coffee-house—Arthur said that she had taken a false oath before the Magistrate, and she said she believed she had—that was alluding to Eliza Weale—I do not remember her saving she had done so to save herself trouble, that she had got into trouble enough already—that might have been said—she said she believed she had taken a false oath—I remember the prosecutrix telling me, after the prisoner was given into custody, that she suspected there was a murdered man in the prisoner's room—she came to the station, and said there was a dreadful stench in the room which the prisoner occupied, and she believed there was something there which should not be, that it smelt as if some dead body was in the room.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Who was present when this matter about the false oath took place? A. Mrs. Burrows, Mr. Dunford, Arthur, and myself and Mr. Davis—it was at the time I went to see what caused the stench—I believe Mrs. Burrows and Arthur began the conversation—I cannot tell what was first said—I did not take particular notice what it was all about—it was respecting some woman she had had taken up a day or two before—I believe the false oath was in reference to that woman—Arthur said he thought that Eliza Weale had taken a false oath, not that Mrs. Burrows had—that is what I mean, and that was what I said before—Arthur said to Mrs. Burrows that Eliza Weale had taken a false oath before
the Magistrate—I did not understand him to say that Mrs. Burrows had—Arthur is here.
( Receipt read)—"Received, July 2, 1842, from Mr. Joseph Hughes, the sum of 12 l. for board and lodging, from Feb. 8, 1842, to Aug. 9, 1842, which sum pays for lodging and board only to that time, Aug. 9, 1842, and all meals had between the date of this receipt and the said Aug. 9th to be paid for on delivery, which J. A. agrees to. My husband, Samuel Burrows, being at sea, has authorised me, Mergrat Burrows, to pay and receive his accounts; and I further agree to pay Mr. Joseph Hughes 18 d. per week for the loan of a bedstead, bed, wool palliasse, pillows and appertenances, from April 5, 1842.— Mergrat Burrows, as per receipt above, for board and lodging, 12 l.— Mergrat Bur"—( the remainder of the name was torn or burnt off.)
MRS. BURROWS re-examined. I always spell my name Margaret —I never signed my name at the bottom of a blank piece of paper with a stamp on it—I never signed my name to any paper for the prisoner, the corner of which was burnt off, and then signed it again—I never gave him a receipt of any kind—I never received 12 l. from him, nor any money whatever, not a farthing.
MR. PHILLIPS called
HENRY DUNFORD . I am an auctioneer and house-agent, and live at Greenwich. I know the prosecutrix from a very slight acquaintance since the 9th of July—I was employed in the first distress at that time, as the appraiser—about the 10th or 11th of this month I saw her at her own house—she said to me, "If Hughes will give up the receipt and pay me the difference, I won't hurt him; if Hughes behaves well to me, and I have 10 l., I will go into the country and squash the concern"—I cannot say that she said, "You know, Mr. Dunford, a word before the Grand Jury will do it"—this was at the second distress—she never told me that the prisoner owed her 12 l.—I was present when the agreement was made to redeem the goods—that was the time this occurred.
MR. PAYNE. Q. When was this conversation between you and Mrs. Burrows? A. On the 9th, 10th, or 11th of August—I did not take particular notice—I had occasion to go there—I had a conversation with her respecting the moving a sippy-table, and in the conversation this came out.
( The Jury, on examining the date on the stamp, stated it to be 18—7, meaning the 18 th of 7 th mouth, July, which was subsequent to the date of the receipt.)
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined Two Years.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18420822-2542]
2542. MARY POWELL was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of August, 3 pairs of stockings, value 6 s.; 1 scarf, value 5 s.; 6 handkerchiefs, value 1 l. 17 s. 6 d.; 9 napkins, value 9 s.; 1 towel, value 1 s.; 1 yard of muslin, value 1 s.; 2 pairs of drawers, value 5 s.; 1 pincushion cover, value 1 s.; and 1 yard of lace, value 1 s.; the goods of Richard Stewart Evelyn Forster, clerk, her master.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
ABABELLA CROW . I am in the service of the Rev. Mr. Forster—the prisoner was his housekeeper—he was in Lincolnshire at the latter end of May—the prisoner went down there, and returned about the 24th of June
—some time after I saw in her conduct something that excited my suspicion—on Sunday, the 31st of July, I went up stairs to her bed-room door, I found it was locked and the key talc en away, which was unusual—I got a key and opened it, and in the room I found some drawers trimmed with lace, and some other articles which I did not believe were the prisoner's—on the 6th of August I told my master and mistress of it.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. How long had you been in the service? A. Two years and three months—the prisoner was put as housekeeper over me—I did not dislike that—I have expressed some dislike because I saw some things which were not proper—I did not go to Lincolnshire this year—the prisoner went with the family, leaving me behind—I opened the prisoner's room door, because I thought things were not right—I had not mentioned it to my master or mistress before—they were not in the house—I had been suspecting things were not right for about three weeks—I never said I would get the prisoner transported nor anything of the kind, nor that I wished she might be transported—I never said anything about it, nor heard anything said about it, before she was taken, and then Bodley, my fellow-servant, said she deserved to be transported.
MR. DOANE. Q. Was it a false key with which you opened the door of the prisoner's room? No, the key of the next room door.
MARY BODLEY . I am servant to Mr. Forster—I had only been there about two months—I observed the prisoner to lock her room door, and she was the only person who did so—on the 7th of August I observed her go towards the water-closet several times within a short period, and as she went on one occasion I observed a piece of muslin hanging from the pockethole of her dress—I then went into the water-closet and searched—I found some muslin, a cap border, and some other articles—they were cleansed and shown to my master and mistress.
Cross-examined. Q. Who directed your attention to the prisoner as she was going to the water-closet? A. No one—Crow was then gone to church—it was Crow who told me the prisoner's locking her door was unusual—I went into the water-closet immediately after the prisoner came out—I was standing on the stain as she came out—I found the articles down the pan quite concealed, so that they would have been destroyed—Crow and I had been talking about this a few evenings before that—I forget whether Crow told me that she had opened the prisoner's room door with another key—she said she went into her room when she was gone to church—I do not know when my master returned from Lincolnshire—I was not then in the service—I said I thought the prisoner deserved to be transported, as Crow said she had things belonging to her master and mistress—we were all bound to tell our mistress.
MR. DOANE. Q. How soon did you tell your mistress? A. On the Monday, the next day.
JOHN DUNCAN . I have been in the service of the Rev. Mr. Forster for twenty-two years. On Monday, the 8th of August, I was requested by him to go up stairs to the prisoner's room—I went up, and found the prisoner, and the ladies'-maid, there—I told the prisoner she had got some of Mr. Forster's property, and to take all the things out of her box, and lay them on the bed—she did so—I then said she had better put her own things back, and give Mr. Forster's things to me—she then gave me these things—I told her to look her own things up, and we should be
obliged to tend for a policeman—I sent for the officer, and delivered the things the prisoner had given me as belonging to my master.
Cross-examined. Q. Where were the things when you went up? A. In her box—she had the lid open, and was at the things.
WILLIAM SMITH ( police-constable R 254.) I took the prisoner, and have the things I received from Duncan.
REV. STEWART EVELYN FORSTER . I am a clergyman of the Church of England, and reside at Lewishan. I had occasion to go into Lincolnshire, where I had resigned two livings which I held there—I had a sister died a short time before, (Mrs. Charles Forster,) and I bad a box which contained various articles of hers, which were kept in Lincolnshire for a niece of hers—I took the prisoner down for the purpose of packing up the wearing-apparel, which I was removing from there to my present residence—she had access to every thing—in consequence of what my servants told me, I sent Duncan up stairs, and be produced these things—I can speak to these stockings very particularly, knowing them to have belonged to my brother—they were marked "C F"—I can see that the C has been taken out, and in this instance the stalk of the "F" is of a different colour to the new work—the box from which these were taken contained other articles of a similar description—this handkerchief I can identify, as being a handsome handkerchief—I have one from the box like it—I cannot say How many there were, but I can say that several have been abstracted—the box is much, lower than it was, in respect to the qnantity of things it contains—these other articles I cannot speak to—here are handkerchiefs which have been recently marked "M P"—they have been lying some time in the box, and they correspond.
Cross-examined. Q. Which are the articles you identify? A. These three pairs of stockings, and the bottoms of these two pain of ladies' drawers—they were the bottom of a baby's robe—it was chiefly baby-linen that was in that box, except pocket-handkerchiefs and stockings—I saw them in the box on the 14th or 15th of June, and I left Lincolnshire about the 20th—I did not take an inventory, nor count the articles in it—I looked in, but did not ascertain whether any articles were missing—I never gave the prisoner any stockings for herself—I gave her some for the narsery-maid, but they were marked with different initials—the box came from Lincolnshire with the rest of my things—I saw all the articles as soon as they arrived at my house—the prisoner was in my service eight months, as cook and housekeeper—she came from Mrs. Barr's boarding-house, at Cheltenham.
MR. DOANE. Q. Did you ever give her stockings of this description? A. No—I never gave her these drawers which are trimmed with lace—she had no right to have touched them.
ELIZABETH NEATH . I am lady's-maid to Mrs. Forster. I remember Duncan coming into the prisoner's room—I have heard the account he has given—it is correct—the fringe that is sewn on to the bottom of this scarf I had missed—it was some that belonged to my master—since the things came from Lincolnshire, I have missed napkins similar to these—I believe them to be the same—there has been "C F" marked on them—it is picked out, but the remains of it is quite plain.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you go up before Duncan? A. I went up with him—I do not know whether I went into the room before him.
JANE BROWN . My husband is the prosecutor's coachman. I remember
the prisoner's returning from Lincolnshire—she came to me soon after for my child to mark and sew for her—I consented to it—I saw my daughter in her room marking the things.
MARY BROWN . I went to the prisoner's room, at Mr. Forster's to mark the things—I marked some stockings, and some other things with the letters "M P."
MRS. FORSTER. I had some fringe of a rose colour—I had it dyed black—it did not take very well—I believe this fringe on this scarf to be part of that fringe—I missed a silk hat-band of my husband's, and this scarf has been a hat-band, I have no doubt—it is a peculiar kind of silk, and here are the remains of "C F" on these napkins, and "M P" is on them now.
Cross-examined. Q. I suppose you scarcely speak with certainty to this fringe? A. Yes, I do—I saw it not many days before I left Lincolnshire—it is worth 4 s. or 5 s.—it was packing up when I was going from Lincolnshire—on the 6th of August the prisoner came to me, and asked me to search her boxes, and they were searched.
MR. DOANE. Q. Do you find the remains of the rose-colour on this identical fringe? A. I do—I did not authorise her to take any of these things.
GUILTY. Aged 28.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury .— Confined Twelve Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

2544. MARY LUNN was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of August, 2 gowns, value 5 s.; and 1 petticoat, value 4 s.; the goods of Eliza Harper; and 1 gown, value 3 s., the goods of Louisa Matthews.
CATHERINE RAWLEY . There was a fire at Deptford on the 9th of August, and I saw the prisoner take a bundle from the premises of Mrs. Gill—I set Edward Smith to watch her.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What time of day was it? A. About eleven o'clock in the morning—I have known the prisoner by sight—her husband is dead, and she has a family—she took these things out of a garden, where they were thrown for safety.
EDWARD SMITH . I watched the prisoner—she took the bundle to a shop in Church-street.
RUTH HARPER . I keep a shop in Church-street. The prisoner came and asked me to let her leave this bundle there—I gave it to the officer.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you known her? A. Yes, for ten years.
WILLIAM SHUTTLE WORTH ( police-constable R 133.) I produce the bundle, which contains these articles, which I got from Mrs. Harper.
LOUISA MATTHEWS . This gown is mine—it was at Mrs. Gill's to be mangled.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know this petticoat? A. Yes—it is my mistress's sister's.
SARAH HARE . I found the petticoat on the prisoner's person.
MARY GILL . I am a laundress. These things were at ray house—they belonged to Eliza Harper and Louisa Matthews—I think the prisoner was in very great distress.
GUILTY. Aged 40.— Recommended to mercy by the jury .— Confined Ten Days.
[trialAccount t18420822-2545]
2545. ANTHONY BURNHAM and MARTHA BURNHAM were indicted for stealing, on the 7th of May, 1 bed, value 3 l.; 1 bolster, value 6 s.; 2 blankets, value 13 s.; and 1 sheet, value 6 s.; the goods of Thomas Seers, the master of Anthony Burnham.
DOROTHY SEERS . I am the wife of Thomas Seers, the master of Anthony Burnham. These articles were in a cottage, which was not ours, but the articles were—the servant saw them there safe last November—they are here, and are ours.
Cross-examined by MR. JONES. Q. I believe you and your husband kept the Tiger's Head at Lee? A. Yes—we left It two years ago, and then we removed these things to the cottage, which we hired for the purpose of putting these things in—we had the key generally, but Anthony Burnham had it occasionally, to go there for different things—he and his wife lived near there—this bolster is my own making, and this bed was made by myself and servants—here is a small hole in this bolster, about sufficient for a pea to pass through—I made the bolster perhaps fourteen or fifteen years ago—I know the ticking of this bed perfectly, and part of my own work is on it—Anthony Burnham has been in our service about twelvemonths.
MARY WINCE . I am in the prosecutors, service. I saw the property safe in the cottage about six months ago.
JOHN CARPENTER ( police-constable R 84.) I went to the prisoner's house, and found Martha Burnham there—it has been rumoured that she is not the wife of Anthony Burnham—I found this bed and bolster there—she said she bought it seven years ago; after that, that it was given her by her mother; and after that, that she bought the tick of a tally man, and the feathers were given her by her mother.
WILLIAM KIRBY ( police-constable R 22.) I went to the prisoner's house with Carpenter, and saw Martha Burnham attempt to conceal something in a black bag—I took the bag from her, and found in it six duplicates, one of which is for a blanket pawned on the 7th of May, which has been identified by Mrs. Seers—I then went after Anthony Burnham—I asked if he had heard any thing from Lee—he said, "No"—I told him Martha Burnham was taken, and cautioned him as to what he said—he said, "Pretty doirgs these are"—he said he had had the bed some years, that he bought it of a man named Distant, who had lived in Theobald's-road some years ago, but was since dead.
EDWARD NASH . I am a pawnbroker. This blanket was pawned by Martha Burnham, on the 7th of May.
MRS. SEERS. This is my blanket—I am able to swear to it.
Cross-examined. Q. How do you know it? A. By its being like the one I lost—I have no mark on it.
ANTHONY BURNHAM — GUILTY. Aged 40.— Recommended to mercy .
— Confined Three Months.
MARTHA BURNHAM — NOT GUILTY .

2547. JOHN SMALE and RICHARD RIVERS were indicted for stealing, on the 20th of July, 6 tame rabbits, price 12. s., the property of William Pershouse, the younger; and that Smale had been before convicted of felony.
WILLIAM PERSHOUSE, JUN . I live with my father, who is a farmer at Greenwich. On the 20th of July I had six tame rabbits in a butch it three o'clock in the afternoon, and at six o'clock the next morning I missed them—I have seen them since—these are them—they are mine—Smile worked for my father.
JOHN ROWSELL . I live in Edward-street, Woolwich, and deal in rabbits. About half-past eight o'clock in the morning of the 21st of July, the prisoners came to me, and I bought these rabbits of them.
GEORGE SALE ( police-constable R 174.) On the 22nd of July I apprehended the prisoner Smale—I told him what it was for—be said he knew nothing about it—I took him to the prosecutor—he told Mr. Pershouse where the rabbits were—he told me—I went and got them.
BENJAMIN LOVELL . I produce a certificate of Smale's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
SMALE — GUILTY . Aged 19.— Transported for Seven Years.
RIVERS — GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined Three Months.

2548. SARAH SCUDDER was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of July, 1 watch, value 7 l.; 1 ribbon, value 1 s.; 3 seals, value 2 l.; 1 key, value 6 d.; the goods of John Black, from his person.
JOHN BLACK . I live in Salisbury-street, Strand. On the 9th of July, I was in the Lewisham-road, Greenwich—I had been dining at Blackwall, and was going home a little heated with wine—the prisoner was standing at the corner of a court—she hailed me, and asked me for money—I gave her either one or two sixpences—the policeman came up and she darted off—he ran after her—I felt and ray watch was gone—this now produced is it.
SAMUEL ARGENT ( police-constable R 54.) I saw the prisoner run up Lewisham-lane—I ran after her, and took this watch, ribbon, seals, and key from her bosom.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Nine Months.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18420822-2549]
2549. DANIEL BOYD was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of July, 2 pairs of trowsers, value 1 l. 16 s., and 1 waistcoat, value 1 l. 4 s.; the goods of a man whose name is unknown.—2nd COUNT, stating them to be the goods of Mark Wells.
SARAH WELLS . I am the wife of Mark Wells, who keeps the Red Cow, in Old King-street, Deptford—I had some trowsers and other things in my care, about six months ago, left by Charles Price, who was mate of a vessel—I put them into a carpet bag, and kept them into a bed-chamber in my house—I missed them on the 29th of July—I know they were safe on
the Sunday before that—on the 28th the prisoner, who I had not known before, came intoxicated and requested a bed for the night, and he had one—he slept in a back room—he went to bed about four o'clock in the afternoon, and rose about twelve the next day—this bag and pair of trowsers were on a box in his room.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Was not he very drank? A. Not so drunk but he could walk up stairs—my husband was aroused by him about three o'clock in the morning to get him some ale or beer—Mr. Price was the chief officer of the Maitland, which is gone to China—I have seen him wear these trowsers many times—I saw them safe on the 24th, and on the 29th I missed them—the door was not kept locked, but I have no one in the house—these are like the trowsers that were there, bat I would not swear to anything that is not mine.
JAMES KNIGHT ( police-constable R 308.) I apprehended the prisoner and took him to the station—Mr. Wells made the charge against him—the sergeant cautioned him, that whatever he said would be used against him—he said, "I have stolen the things, and thrown them in the mouth of the cuddy."
Cross-examined. Q. What time of day was this? A. About a quarter before nine in the evening—I could not say he was drunk—I did not write down the words he said—we did not say it would be better to tell the truth—I went and found these articles in the boatswain's chest, in the cuddy—here are two pairs of trowsers and a waistcoat.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Baron Rolfe.
[trialAccount t18420822-2550]
2550. JAMES WHITTLE and GEORGE WHITE were indicted, for that they, on the 3rd of August, upon John Hoare, did feloniously make an, assault, and put him in fear and danger of his life, and take from his person, and against his will, 1 watch, value 3 l. 18 s.; 1 watch-chain, value 2 d.; 1 watch-key, value 1 d.; 1 seal, value 1 d.; 1 knife, value 1 s.; 1 tobacco-box, value 6 d.; 1 handkerchief, value 6 d.; and 6 halfpence; his property: and immediately before, at the time, and immediately after the said robbery, did cut and wound him.
MR. PHILLIPS conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN HOARE . I am a gardener, living near Borstall-bill, in the parish of Plumstead, in Kent. On Wednesday, the 3rd of August, between twelve and one o'clock in the morning, I was going towards my cottage—I met two men—I should not know them again—they knocked me down—I felt them take something from me, but I soon lost my senses—when I came to my senses I missed my watch, knife, handkerchief, tobacco-box, and threepence-halfpenny in copper—I had been at a public-house—I was sober—I saw my watch again on the 4th of August, a person belonging to the 73rd regiment showed it me—I saw the handkerchief again that day week—I had been drinking for three hours at the public-house—I had last seen my watch at past twelve o'clock—that public-house was about half a mile from my house—this happened in the parish of Plumstead.
Whittle. Q. Can you say I am the person that robbed you? A. No.
GEORGE BAKER ( police-constable R 60.) I was on duty on Tuesday night, the 2nd of August, and about half-past eleven o'clock I saw the two prisoners quarrelling by the Arsenal wall—I told them if they did not go away I should have to go and fetch the piquet—Whittle said they were
going for a country walk, down to the Green Man, at Plumstead—I saw them go in the direction for Plumstead—I saw them again at half-past six on the following morning, very near the Standard, in Woolwich—I only know them by seeing them about the street at various times—I have no doubt as to their being the persons.
Whittle. Q. What time did you see me first? A. I had seen you several nights.
MARY ANDERSON . I live in Woolwich. On Tuesday, the 2nd of August, the two prisoners were in the Royal Standard skittle-ground, at Woolwich, playing at skittles—they went into a public-house—I staid there till the house was shut up—I went in at four o'clock, and left about half-past ten—they were there all that time—about seven the next morning I went to the same public-house, and found the prisoners there—Whittle asked me whether I would show them where the pawn-shop was—he said he wanted to go and pawn his watch—I went with them as far as the pawnbroker's door—Whittle went in, and White stopped outside—I returned to the public-house again, after that, with them, and staid there till eleven o'clock, when a piquet came to take them away.
CHARLES HIRONS ( police-constable R 228.) I was in duty in Woolwich about ten minutes to five o'clock in the morning of the 3rd of August—I saw the prisoners in Nightingale-vale, coming from Plumsteadcommon—White asked me which was the way to Woolwich—they had turned up a wrong turning, and came back—I am quite sure they are the two men.
PATRICK COLEMAN . I am a private in the 73rd Regiment of Foot On Wednesday evening, the 3rd of August, I was sentry at the Royal Artillery Barracks at Woolwich—I saw Harris search Whittle—I got a handkerchief from White, and asked him if it was his—he said no—I asked him if it belonged to his comrade—he said no—I kept it—this is it—( producing it)—the duplicate of a watch was found on Whittle—White was in confinement, under military punishment—the prisoners were privates in the 73rd Regiment, which has been at Woolwich seven or eight months—I never saw the prisoners till that time, to my knowledge.
GEORGE HARRIS ( police-constable R 158.) In consequence of some information, on the afternoon of Wednesday, the 3rd of August, I went to the barracks, and took Whittle into custody—I told him he was charged with robbing a man of his watch, and assaulting him on the Queen's highway—he said he was quite innocent of the charge—I searched him, and found, in his left-hand trowsers' pocket, the duplicate of a watch, pawned for 20 s.—I said, "Here is a duplicate, what duplicate is this?"—he said, "It is my own watch, I have had it for a long time"—I found two handkerchiefs on him—(one was very bloody indeed, which I found in his breast,) and 4 s. 6 d. in silver—I then went in search of White—I found him in a bed half an hour after—I searched him, and found on him a comb and a handkerchief.
MICHAEL BROWN ( police-inspector R.) The prisoners were brought to me, on the evening of the 3rd of August, at the station—I read the charge to them, which was for robbing a man—White said, in answer to the corporal who brought him from the barracks, that he left Whittle at half-past eleven o'clock, and then he had no watch—he said he slept in the same house, at Woolwich, but he could not tell where that was—Whittle's hands were all over blood—I asked him about it, and he said it was blood from his nose—his trowsers were very much stained with blood—there was a particular
stain about the right knee, as though he had been kneeling down, and some marks of blood on the left knee of them.
White. He has said things I never said; I said no such words. Witness. Yes, he did, in the presence of the corporal—I believe Harris heard the same words.
ROBERT HUNTER . I live with Mr. Moore, a pawnbroker, at Woolwich. Whittle pledged this watch at our shop about eight o'clock in the morning of the 3rd of August—I gave him this duplicate—it is pledged in his own name, for 1 l., and the number of his regiment is put, the "73rd"—this is what he described himself.
THOMAS WRIGHT . I am a surgeon, at Woolwich. Early in the morning of the 3rd of August, I examined the prosecutor, and found him in a weak state from the loss of blood—I found the mark of a blow on his forehead, which I should say was inflicted with the fist—there was only one mark, but it was of very considerable size—there was blood on his face—the hair of his head was completely matted with blood, and there was a wound on the back part of the head, between the ears—the blood might have come from that wound—it was a wound about two inches in length—it appeared to have been made with a bludgeon, or it might possibly have been a kick—there were no other wounds.
JURY. Q. Could that wound arise from a fall backwards? A. Yes, but it appeared as if it had been from a blow with a stick.
COURT. Q. Was it such a cut as might have resulted not from a blow at all, but from a blow in the front knocking him down? A. Decidedly not.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Whittle's Defence. The watch I had was mine, and I have had it eight or nine years; I am innocent.
White. I hope you will have mercy on me; I am innocent.
WHITTLE — GUILTY . Aged 20.
WHITE — GUILTY . Aged 25.
DEATH Recorded.

2552. HERBERT GOMPERTZ was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of June, 1 sovereign, 4 half-crowns, 6 sixpences, 36 pence, and 134 halfpence, the monies of Robert Henry Davies, his master.
HOBERT HENRY DAVIES . I live in Fletcher-row, Clerkenwell. In June last I kept a beer-shop at Vauxhall—the prisoner was in the habit of coming there—on the 26th of June I left him in charge of the premises between two and three o'clock, locked the desk, took the key with me, and left the money stated in the indictment, safe—I went out to tea and left him to mind the business; his master, next door, being a pious man, I thought I could entrust him—I returned at half-past ten in the evening—I found
no gas alight and the place locked up—I was obliged to get through the next house to get in—I found two policemen at the door, and the key of the gas-meter box taken out and used to force open the desk, and the money gone—a pair of scissors had also been used—I found the key in the desk bent—he never returned—he was apprehended a month afterwards.
Prisoner. He forced the desk open himself, a week before, when he lost the key. Witness. I did not—I never lost the key.
Prisoner. The prosecutor was aware I was going down to the country on Monday; he only left 29 s. 6 d. in the desk for change, and left the key in it; he has got up this prosecution to extort money; he has endeavoured to get my friends to advance money—my brother gave him 3 s.—he owed his brewer 3 l. 4 s., and asked me to take out a loan in my name, and I should have half of it; he said as I was going to leave, they could not come on me for payment, and as he was going to leave, they would not find him; I refused. Witness. It is not so—out of feeling towards his father and mother I would not bring them as witnesses—the prisoner was apprehended at the workhouse when he came there—he was very badly off before this, he wanted shoes—I said if he chose to get a loan I would be security for 5 l., but would not allow him to have more than half the money until I saw How he went on—it was to get him clothes, as I knew if he got sixpence he would spend it in drink—he had 12 s. a-week from his master—the loan was to be for himself—I took the key of the desk with me—I also lost money from the till.
AGATHA BECKMAN . I live in Vauxhall-street—I went to Davies's house for a pot of beer about three o'clock—the prisoner served me—I saw the desk open then—he said he was going to enjoy himself—when I came out he shut the door after me—it was open before.
WELLINGTON WEBSTER . I am a policeman—I took the prisoner into custody on the 26th of July—he admitted taking 29 s. from the desk, but disputed the full amount.
Prisoner. Q. Did not I say there was but 29 s. left in the desk, without admitting taking any? A. You said you took 29 s.—I am certain I found the desk broken open when I was sent for—a pair of scissors and a key were given me, with which it appeared to have been forced open—the prisoner said the prosecutor was never worth 3 l., but he admitted taking the money.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined Six Mouths.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

2553. EDWARD HUGHES was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of August, 1 hand-barrow, value 1 l.; 300 herrings, value 10 s.; 2 baskets, value 1 s.; the goods of Samuel Jones, his master; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
SAMUEL JONES . I deal in coals, and live in Neptune-street, Newington—on the 4th of August I employed the prisoner, who said he was in distress, to take out some herrings to sell—I gave him a barrow with 300 herrings to sell—he was to account for them—I gave him sixpence the night before to get victuals with, as he said he was in distress, and I gave him another sixpence when he went away with the barrow and herrings—he never brought them or the money back—I saw nothing more of him until he was in custody on the 13th—on the 19th, since he was committed, the barrow has been left at my house.
Prisoner. You agreed to lend me the money and was to go partners with me; I was to go and sell the things. Witness. I employed you, and was to pay you for your day's work when you returned.
JAMES BROOK . I am a policeman—I took the prisoner in charge on Saturday the 13th—I told him the charge—he said he sent the barrow home by a boy they called " Fishey," that he sold the herrings for 10 s.—he said the boy's father worked at the gas-woks—I inquired there, and found the boy is about eight years old.
Prisoner's Defence. I returned the barrow on Monday evening by a boy who is eighteen years of age; he could not find the house out—I was remanded when I was taken, but could not find him—my brother has since found him and returned the barrow to Mr. Jones.
EDWARD ELLIOTT . I am a policeman. I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from the office of the Clerk of the Peace for Surrey—( read)—I was present at the trial—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY .* Aged 27.— Confined Twelve Months.

2554. HENRY TAYLOR was indicted for feloniously assaulting Patrick Keefe, on the 31st of July, putting him in bodily fear, and taking from his person, and against his will, I watch, value 1 l. 8 s.; 1 watch-key, value 6 d.; 1 seal, value 6 d.; and 1 watch-chain, value 6 d.; his goods; and before, at the time of, and after, feloniously beating, striking, and using other personal violence to him.
PATRICK KEEFE . I am an upholsterer, and live at Pleasant-place, Pitt-street, St. George's-road, Southwark. On the night of the 31st of July, about eleven o'clock, I was returning home in St. George's-road, and was suddenly knocked down, and, when down, I found the prisoner in the act of taking my watch out of my pocket—I called out "Police," and tried to take the watch from him—he held the Watch, and I held the seals, until Lee came up—there were two persons attacked me—the one who got away knocked me down, and the prisoner took my watch—I am certain he is the man—the watch produced is mine—I am certain.
Prisoner. Q. You say I had the watch in my hand, and you had the seals in your hand? A. Yes—Lee came and secured you.
RICHARD LEE . I am a boot and shoe-maker, and live at No. 17, Gable-street, Southwark. I saw the prosecutor in St. George's-road, walking steadily along—he was knocked down by the prisoner's companion—as soon as he was down, the prisoner snatched the watch from hi fob—he got hold of the seals, and the prisoner had the watch in his hand.
RICHARD PROSSER ( police-constable L 53.) I received charge of the prisoner, and have the watch.
Prisoners Defence. I was returning home and heard a call for a policeman—I went over, and saw the gentleman lying in the road—I was walking away, and the constable laid bold of me, and said I must go with him—I never touched the watch.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Transported for Fifteen Years.
[trialAccount t18420822-2555]
2555. JOHN WHEELER was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of July at Battersea, 14 sovereigns, the monies of Thomas Smith, his master, in his dwelling-house.
THOMAS SMITH . I keep the White Hart at Battersea—the prisoner was my pot-boy. On the 27th of July, about half-past ten o'clock in the morning, I missed fourteen sovereigns from my till—the key of the kitchen door will unlock the bar-door, but I did not know it then—I saw my money safe at half-past six that morning—the till was locked—there are two tills, one inside the other—the inner one goes within four inches of the bottom—this money was between the two tills—when we want the money to change, the inner till is taken out, and then we get to the gold and silver—there was 9 l. 3 s. in silver in a bag, and 27 l. 10 s. in gold—I lost no silver—there was a shilling dropped in the bar—I have not found any of the gold—I found the till still locked—if we go out, the till is locked, and the key put between two liquor-tubs—it was found there—I keep the key of the bar in my pocket—I charged the prisoner as soon as the money was missed.
MARY COOPER . I am in the service of Mr. Smith. On the morning of the 27th of July, about a quarter to seven o'clock, the prisoner cue into the kitchen—he asked where master was—I said he was outside—he said no more, but went and took the key of the parlour door, and tried the bar door—he said that was no use—then he went to the kitchen-door—he said, "This is the one"—he then went into the bar—I saw him stand with his hands close to the till—when he came out, he put his hand into his right-hand pocket, and said, "All I have taken is two cigars"—master was in the yard at the time, at work—mistress had not come out of her room.
Prisoner. She came and asked me to come and sit down by the side of her, and asked me if I had unlocked the bar. Witness. No, I never spoke to him—I did not ask him why he wanted the key to open the bar-door—I was alarmed when I saw it—when mistress came down and missed the money, she asked me, and I said I had not seen any money—I was taken up on suspicion—I did not tell my master of it.
JOHN PAYNE ( police-constable V 11.) The prisoner and Cooper were both brought to the Wandsworth station—Cooper said several times to the prisoner, "John, you have got the money, why don't you give it up?"—I told her not to say anything—she said, "He has got the money, I saw him go in"—he made no answer.
JOSEPH TIGHE ( police-constable V 199.) I received change of the prisoners—on the way to the station, Cooper said in the prisoner's presence, "I have not got the money, John has got it, I saw him at the till"—she said, "John, tell me where the money is, and I will go back and get it, and spare all further trouble"—he made no reply—I found 2 l. 4 s. 6 d. in silver, and 10 1/2 d. in copper on the prisoner, and 1 s. 4 d. on Cooper about an hour after the robbery was discovered.
Prisoner. The money that was taken from me, was money I had by me for setting up skittles, and 3 s. a week for wages—I was two months and three weeks with him, and 18 s. I had for a watch that was raffled at the Waterman's Arms, Mr. Prior's.
SAMUEL JORDAN . I had a game at skittles with the prisoner some time before the robbery—I lost 1 s.—I asked him to lend me one—he said he had only 2 1/2 d.—I cannot tell How long this was before the robbery—I suppose about u month—I know that he raffled a watch—I cannot tell whether it was his own or not—I know he had one, and he sold it by a raffle.
Prisoner's Defence. He had 1 s. worth of halfpence, and if you lend him anything, he will not pay you again.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Justice William.

2556. WILLIAM BAVIN was indicted for feloniously assaulting James Nash, on the 20th of August, and cutting and wounding him in and upon the head, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
Mr. Charnoch conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES NASH . I am a chair and sofa maker, and live in Broadwall, Stamford-street, Surrey—the prisoner was my journeyman. On Saturday night last, about eleven o'clock, he came to be settled with—he produced his account to me—there was a difference between us as to the charges, which were more than I thought I should pay—he called me a b—ferret-eyed looking b—, and a shuffling trickster, said said he should like to give me something—I then sent for a policeman, who heard me tell him, what he had done I would pay him for, and when the other work was finished, I would settle for that—he then appeared more calm, and the constable left—I proceeded to settle with him—he went and sat at the end of the room, and I was settling with the other men—while I was sitting down writing, he got up, came behind me, and in an instant I felt a tremendous crash on my head, three times; had I not put my hand up, to save the second blow, I believe death must have been the consequence, for I received that blow on my hand instead of my head—I cannot say who struck the blow—I became insensible, and recollect nothing more till I found myself reviving in the surgeon's shop—I was afterwards attended by the surgeon of St. Thomas's Hospital—I managed to go out a little way on Sunday evening, but felt so faint I was obliged to return.
WILLIAM BUCHANAN . I am in the employ of Mr. Nash—I was there on Saturday evening while he and the prisoner were settling some accounts—there was a dispute with the prisoner—a policeman was sent for, and he became quiet—after that he sat down in the room by himself—the prosecutor began to settle with another man—I saw the prisoner get off his seat, walk round behind the prosecutor, take an empty champagne bottle off the drawers, and strike the prosecutor over the head with it as hard as he could, apparently—master had his hat off, and was sitting at the table—he struck him three times with it—he said nothing—the prosecutor got up and went out—he staggered outside the door—his head bled a good deal—the bottle was broken all to pieces—I think it broke with the second blow, and the third was given with the neck of it—I ran to master's assistance, went over to the doctor's to him, and saw him sitting, bleeding in a chair—he was sensible then—the prisoner was quite sober—I gave him in charge.
JAMES MURRAY . I am a carpenter, and joiner. I live at No. 2, Black-horse-court, Farringdon-street. On Saturday evening last, I was at Mr. Wash's house, and heard a dispute about the prisoner's account—I went in with the policeman, who left—the prosecutor was sitting at the table—the prisoner took up a bottle—he pushed a man against me as I stood in the passage, to get at the bottle—he was behind Mr. Nash—he struck Mr. Nash three times across the head with it, as hard as he could—part of the
bottle flew over me—the last blow was struck with the neck of it—Nash bled, and was taken to a doctor's—the prisoner came out of the room—I seized him, and gave him to the policeman—he was perfectly sober.
GEORGE SMITH . I am a policeman. On Saturday evening last I was called in to Nash—there were words between him and the prisoner—they were quieter, and before I had got a few yards from the house, I heard a dreadful smash, and a cry of, "Murder and police"—I went back—Nash passed me as I went to the house—the blood was running from his head—I have his clothes here—I took the prisoner into custody.
HENRY PETTNER FREEMAN . I am a surgeon and dresser at St. Thomas's hospital. Nash has been under my care since Tuesday—I examined his head on Tuesday—it had been dressed before I saw it—he had an incised wound, similar to one made by glass, about an inch, or rather more, long—it was only one wound—it extended nearly to the bone—it was not dangerous, but might have become so—he complained of pain in his head, but had no fever—I saw the fragments of the bottle—it appears to be a common champagne bottle—I consider that a dangerous sort of instrument.
Prisoner's Defence. He refused to pay my wages; I told him that was wrong; he got up, seized hold of me, and told me to protect myself, I got hold of the bottle, and hit him twice over the head.
GUILTY.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor .— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Recorder.

2557. EDWARD THOMAS was indicted for breaking and entering the counting-house of Thomas Evans and another, on the 13th of August, at St. Saviour, Southwark, and stealing therein 1 pair of spectacles, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 hat, value 2 s.; 1 coat, value 6 s.; the goods of Thomas Evans; and 3 pairs of compasses, value 1 s. 6 d.; 2400 pence, and 4800 halfpence, the property of Thomas Evans and another.
THOMAS EVANS . I am in partnership with John Lascelles, of Great Guildford-street, St. Saviour, Southwark. On the 13th of August, about half-past six in the morning, I went to the premises, and found the counting-house door open, and the desks forced—I missed out of my desk a pair of spectacles, three pairs of compasses, and some loose copper of my own; also some coats, a waistcoat, and hat—the key of the iron-chest, which had been locked up in my desk, was taken out—20 l. in copper belonging to the firm was gone from the chest—I had locked the desk about half-past one the day before—the counting-house is in a yard, at the back of the house of Mr. Burgess, our clerk—it is enclosed by a wall, and there is a door leading to Mr. Lascelles premises from it.
JOHN LASCELLES, JUN . I live with my father, and am employed in the counting-house—about half-past seven or a quarter to eight o'clock, I shut up the counting-house, locked the door, and took the key—it was all safe at nine o'clock.
REDMAN WELSH ( police-constable M 109.) On the 13th of August, about two o'clock in the morning, I was on duty in Guildford-street, Southwark, and saw the prisoner and another—I followed them into Gravel-lane—they saw me, and separated—I followed, stopped the prisoner, and found 6 s. 3 1/2 d. in copper, three pairs of compasses, a pair of spectacles, and case
on him—he was wearing a coat, waistcoat, and hat, which I now produce—be had them on.
MR. EVANS re-examined. This is my coat and waistcoat—they were taken from a box—the lock of the door appeared to have been picked—the other articles are mine.
CHARLES LEWIS . I am a policeman. I took these articles off the prisoner's back.
JOHN MILLS ( police-constable M 31.) I found a coat in the prosecutor's counting-house—I made the prisoner try it on—it appeared to fit him.
Prisoner's Defence. It is not ray coat; I was coming from Horseley-down-stairs, went into a public-house, and bought the things of a man who owned them; I saw the two policemen drinking together when they stopped me.
REDMAN WELSH re-examined. We stopped him 150 yards from the counting-house—we were not drinking—there is no public-house in the street.
Prisoner. A young gentleman in the street with them had got a bottle.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Ten Years.
Before Mr. Baron Rolfe.
