2558. WILLIAM TAYLOR was indicted for feloniously assaulting Robert Collins, on the 22nd of August, and cutting and wounding him on his head, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.—2nd COUNT, stating his intent to be to resist and prevent his lawful apprehension and detainer.
ROBERT COLLINS ( police-constable V 108.) On Monday, the 22nd of August, between six and seven o'clock in the evening I was on duty on Kennington Common—there was a meeting there of three or four thousand persons, I should say—I did not hear any speaking myself—we were ordered clear the Common, and shortly after doing so I saw the prisoner in the Kennington-road, throwing stones at the constables who were on the Common—the last stone he threw struck me on my right hand—I got over the rils, and took hold of him with my left hand—be resisted, but through the bow on my right hand he succeeded in getting my staff from me, and strugged with me in the road—he struck me on the head with my own staff, which stnned me for the moment—I was assisted by the constable to a doctor's—ve were struggling at the time he struck me, and both fell down on the footway—he was underneath—he struck me twice, but only once on the he—the other blow was on the shoulder—I was stunned, and cannot tell who her he struck me after the second time—when I came to myself two conables of the L division had him in custody—I did not see my staff agai till I got to Tower-street station—it was taken from him—the sergean who took the charge gave it me.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What time did you go on duty that right at Kennington Common? A. Shortly after six o'clock—there had on a meeting gathering together for some time before we went there—he people were not allowed to go on the Common after the police were stioned there—there might be 500 police there, as near as I can tell—to sergeants and inspectors were on horseback—the police themselves are not—I have no recollection of seeing a constable on horseback—the meeting had been dispersed several minutes when I saw the prisoner throw she at the police—the police had nothing to drink on the Common there were no cans of drink brought on the Common for them
—I swear that—they had some beer about six o'clock, when we were stationed in reserve in Kennington-lane—I had none myself—I did not feel inclined to have any—I could have had it if I had liked to purchase it the same as any other constable—any man that liked to have half-a-pint of beer might have had it—I had taken nothing since my tea—I had not been drinking in the course of the day—I had been in bed all day—I never had anything to drink from the time I left home, which was tea, and previous to that I never tasted malt liquor of any description—I did not remain at the station-house that night—I went to Kennington-lane after this happened—I did not to my knowledge see the prisoner's wife come to Tower-street station after this happened—I was not singing and keeping it up at the station after I had received the blow on my head—I felt too unwell—I did not sing at the station that night, while the prisoner was in custody—I remained at the station about a quarter of an hour after the prisoner was locked up—I never sat down there after he was given in charge—the prisoner was standing on the high road from Kennington-gate to the Horns when he was throwing the stones, on the right hand side from Brixton, the side nearest the Common—there were a great many people standing there—they extended across the road—it is a wide road—I was standing on the Common, not more than a dozen yards from the railings—the prisoner was standing just outside the railings—I cannot recollect who the persons were on each side of him—I saw other men, but seeing him amuse himself with throwing stones, he took my attention more than anybody—I seized him with my left hand, holding my staff in my right—he seized my staff—we scuffled for about a minute—I did not get the better of him—he fell down—it was not in the act of falling under me that he struck the blows—it was just as he was going, with his back towards the path—he was in the act of falling when be struck me—it was after the scuffle—we bad a scuffle of course when we were down—I swear that I had not received the blow on my head, on the Common before I say the prisoner—it was a blow from my own staff from him—I was the first person that seized on the prisoner—it was not Lutsford—I laid hold I him before Lutsford got over the rails—Lutsford came up two minutes afterwards—the greater part of the people were on the Common at the time the prisoner was throwing the stones.
HENRY LUTSFORD ( police-constable L 87.) I was at Kenningon Common last Monday evening—there were a number of people assembled there—I assisted in dispersing them—I saw the prisoner standing outside the rails in Kennington-road, just by the edge of the footpath, by that I could see of him—I cannot say whether he was on the path or in the kennel—I saw him standing there among the people—I was working by close to Collins, and I saw the prisoner hoist his hand, and throw a stone on to the Common towards where the police were—the police were just opposite the place where he threw—I was close to Collins at the time—I did not see whether the stone hit him—my eye was fixed on prisoner, and I was about to get over the rails, but Collins was over rails before I was, and had got the prisoner by the collar with his left and—I was about to get through the mob as quick as I could to assist him in taking him, when I saw the prisoner take the truncheon from Collins's right hand—a struggle ensued, and I saw him strike Collins On the head with the truncheon—Collins's hat was off—it fell off in the ruggle, I
believe before he was struck—I saw blood flow—I then laid hold of the prisoner, and we all three fell to the ground together—I rescued Collins's truncheon from the prisoner's hand as he was about to strike him again—another constable came to our assistance, and we took him to the station.
Cross-examined. Q. Was the prisoner in the act of falling at the time the blow was struck? A. I believe he was—we were all in the act of falling together—Smith No. 119, was the other constable—he is not here—I did not see Collins's hat go off—Collins had hold of the prisoner first, not me—I did not remain at Tower-street station all that night—I stopped there till I had lodged the prisoner there, and then I went back to see where Collins was—I had been in reserve in Kennington-lane before the meeting—I saw some beer there, and I had a pint myself, for which I paid twopence—that was all I had—I had had nothing to drink before that except my tea at breakfast—I had my dinner in Keanington-lane—that was when I had the pint of porter—I believe there were cans of beer, not very large ones—the men were not allowed to have what they liked—Mr. Grinsell, the superintendent, did not allow us more than a pint each.
COURT. Q. How long had yon been in reserte there? A. I think from about half-past ten o'clock in the morning, and remained there all day—we paid for the beer ourselves.
JURY. Q. How did you come on the ground? A. Through the rush of the mob on us—I had hold of the prisoner at the time, and we all fell together—a gentleman, who I believe is a witness, came in to our assistance at the time, besides the constable No. 119.
JOHN BROWN . I am a pensioner from the army, and live in Upper Crown-street, Westminster. I am not of any trade or occupation—last Monday evening, I was on the road that leads by the side of Kennington Common, passing in the rear of those who were standing on the footpath, looking over the palings enclosing the Common—I saw the prisoner, not in the dress he is now in, but in a smock-frock, draw his hand with a stone, and throw it into the Common at the police—I did not see what sort of a stone it was—a policeman immediately jumped over the palings like a shot—the prisoner went a little to his left to conceal himself behind the mob—the policeman immediately seized him by the collar with his left hand—the prisoner resisted violently—the mob journeyed about, shoved both the policeman and the prisoner about, and shooed them, I believe, into the middle of the road, where I saw the prisoner and the policeman down on the road, the policeman uppermost, and the prisoner covered over, with a considerable quantity of blood on him, which I thought flowed from himself, but which I found afterwards was from the policeman's head—I told the policeman that that was the man that threw take stone—I was junted about with the mob—I drew a cane, which I had in my hand, in a menacing manner, at the mob, which kept them back till two or three policemen came in and secured the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. In what capacity were you in the army? A. A private soldier, a bombardier, a corporal, a sergeant, a colour-sergeant, and pay-sergeant of a company—I live at Westminster—I happened on this day to be assisting a friend of mine, who is an assessor, to deliver the Income tax papers—I was in front of the Common, and in some few
streets, until nearly the time when this affair occurred—I was not on the Common, but in the road, where there were many hundreds more—I did not see any blows struck by the prisoner—I believe I was the only person who said any thing to the police—I had not come there with any of the police to assist them—I was not hired by the Government—after I had distributed the Income tax papers, I thought I would have a look at these good boys that were going to make gentlemen of us all, and equally divide every thing—I wanted to see what a show these Chartists made.
DAVID TAYLOR . I am a surgeon. Collins was brought to my surgery, and I attended him—he had an ordinary contused wound of the head of a very trifling character—I have no doubt it is well now, or nearly so—I have not seen it, because I have been engaged here during the last week.
JURY. Q. Was the wound of that magnitude as to cover the prisoner with blood? A. Trifling wounds will sometimes produce a large quantity of blood without any serious importance—there was nothing in the nature of the wound to lead me to suppose it impossible that it should cause a good deal of blood.
MR. PAYNE called
JOSEPH HOLDSWORTH . I am a plasterer, and live in Dove-row, Hackney-road. I know the prisoner—on Monday, the 22nd of August, we were working for Mr. Tully at some houses in Lambeth-road, about a quarter of an hour's walk from Kennington-Common—the prisoner left work at six o'clock that evening—I heard him say in the course of the day that he had some tools at some other job, and he should go and fetch them in the evening, and he left with that intention—he only worked with me a week—he seemed a sober, peaceable man.
COURT. Q. Were you at the meeting? A. No.
ROBERT COLLINS re-examined. I saw the prisoner throw eight or nine different stones before the one that struck me—other people were throwing stones after that and before that, but not at the time the stone struck me—the prisoner was standing among the crowd, outside the rails, on the footpath, when he threw the last stone that hit me, and after that he was hiding behind the people—he was at the head of the footpath when he threw four or five, and then he came to the edge of the rails, and threw the last two or three—there were a good many people on the path—I was ten or twelve-yards from the rails—I could see him, being much the taller man there, and having a white frock on—I saw him come forward and throw the stone, and then run back among the crowd—I thought it was time to take him when he struck me—I did not take him before, because I saw the danger of going over the rails singly—I might have got killed, and should, no doubt, but for the assistance of Lutsford.
HENRY LUTSFORD re-examined. The prisoner must have been by the curb of the footpath when he threw the stone—I saw the place on Collins's hand afterwards—I did not see the prisoner standing near the rails at all—I saw Mr. Brown with his cane after the prisoner was in custody—he kept back the crowd in the best manner he could—the crowd pushed us down.
JURY. Q. Is it not possible that Collins might have received the blow from the truncheon of some other policeman? A. There was no other policeman there but me till after he was secured, and when I got bold of the prisoner he had the truncheon in his right hand—I saw that Collins's hand was bruised at the back, which rendered it useless—we were on our logs again when Smith (No. 119) came in—we then took him over the
rails into the common among our men, to secure him there till we got a conveyance—we were obliged to take him away in a cab, the mob was so great—they threatened to rescue him.
JOHN BROWN re-examined. I do not believe I was a yard from the prisoner when the stone was thrown—he had on a white smock-frock and a cap—I have not the least doubt of his throwing the stone—I saw one other man throw a stone a few yards before I came up to the prisoner—the prisoner drew his hand so near my face that I could not help seeing him—I was passing along to leave the ground.
JOSEPH HOLDSWORTH re-examined. I parted with the prisoner at six o'clock that evening—he had a shiny cap and smock-frock on—it was not white—he had been working in it for a month or so.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY on the 2nd Count.— Strongly recommended to mercy on account of his good character .— Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18420822-2559]
2559. MARY ANN NYE was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of August, 1 box, value 5 s.; 1 work-box, value 4 s.; 3 yards of ribbon, value 6 d.; 2 brooches, value 4 s.; 1 printed book, value 6 d.; 2 petticoats, value 5 s.; 11 gowns, value 1 l. 19 s.; 3 pillow-cases, value 2 s.; 1 boa, value 5 s.; 1 pair of stays, value 2 s.; 4 shawls, value 10 s.; 1 cape, value 3 s.; 14 aprons, value 12 s.; 18 handkerchiefs, value 10 s.; 1 pair of boots, value 2 s.; 1 pair of shoes, value 1 s.; 5 pairs of stockings, value 2 s. 6 d.; 3 sheets, value 7 s.; 1 housewife, value 6 d.; 12 napkins, value 3 s.; 2 pairs of cuffs, value 6 d.; 6 spoons, value 2 s.; 1 pair of sugar-tongs, value 6 d.; 8 oz. weight of tobacco, value 2 s.; 24 cigars, value 1 s.; 1 bonnet, value 2 s.; and 8 yards of printed cotton, value 1 s.; the goods of James Illman: and WILLIAM PRATT , for feloniously inciting and procuring the said Mary Ann Nye to commit the said felony.—2nd COUNT, for feloniously receiving the said goods, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES ILLMAN . I keep the Yew Tree public-house, at Reigate—the prisoner Pratt is a working gardener, and has been in the habit of coming to my house as a customer for about twelve months—I did not suspect any thing wrong between him and my wife. On Wednesday night, the 3rd of August, about ten o'clock, I missed my wife from my house, and afterwards missed, a quantity of property—Nye is my wife's cousin, and had been on a visit at Reigate for a short time previous to this—she had not been stopping at my house, but had been there several times—I did not see Nye leave on the 3rd of August—I was out—I came up to London that night, and gave information to the police—I went to the Elephant and Castle, at Newington, a little before eleven next morning, and there found a box of mine, addressed to "Miss Nye"—I waited there all that day and the next, till about two, and the prisoner Nye then came in a cab —she asked for that box, and it was delivered to her—after she had got it, and had got into the cab again, I went up and asked her if she knew what property she had got there—she said yes, it was her own—I called a policeman, and gave her into custody, and he took possession of the box—I afterwards went to the Duke of Wellington public-house—I do not know in what
street it is—it is about a mile and a half from the Elephant and Castle—I there found Pratt, with two umbrellas, a basket, and a bandbox—I gave him into custody, and told him it was for stealing my property—I said nothing about my wife—I left him in charge of the officers, and then went to look for my wife—I found her at the top of that same street.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. How long have you been married? A. About four years—my wife's name was Ann Nye before I married her—she was not a widow—I have never complained of her conduct before with anybody, nor of her absence on any occasion—she has not been living with me since this has happened—she was at my house one night, the night we went down by the night-van late—she remained there with her sister, and next morning she went down to her mother's—I have seen her during the last week—she has been living with her sister, away from me—I have slept in the same house—when Nye came for the box, I asked if she knew what she had got there—she said, "Yes"—I then told her it was my property, and gave her into custody—that was all that took place.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you say anything to Pratt, when you found him? A. I said, "You are a pretty fellow, are you not?"—he said, "What have I done?" and I said, "You have ran away with my wife and all my property"—this was on Friday, the 5th—my wife left home on Wednesday, the 3rd—I had seen Pratt in my house a little before ten o'clock that evening—I was down in the cellar for about ten minutes when my wife left, and I missed her when I came up.
ANN ILLMAN . I am the prosecutor's wife. I left my husband's house with Pratt on the 3rd of August—Nye had been at Reigate for some days before—I did not tell her anything about Pratt's attention to me—he asked me if she should go and pack up my things, and she went up stairs and packed them up—I first conversed with Nye about Pratt about three days before that—she asked me to go away—I did not say that I would or would not—she said if I wanted to go away I was to go with Pratt—I had not told her anything about him before that—he had spoken to her—she told me that they had been talking together, and she wished me to go away with him—I did not exactly agree to that—I did at last go away with him, with a great deal of persuasion by both of them—Pratt asked me what I was going to take with me—I told him I did not know—he said I was to take as much as I could with me, and I was to take as much money as I could, and the watch besides, and leave my husband penniless—I was very much shocked at that proposal—he said he could not take anything more than what he stood upright in, and I was to take as much as I could of my husband's clothes for him—Nye also told me to take as much as I could with me, and she packed the box up herself—Pratt corded it, and wrote the direction on it—it was to go away as her box—she went away about three o'clock that afternoon, with the box, by the coach—Pratt went about two or three hours before her, and met her on the road—that was arranged before they went away—Pratt said he would come and fetch me, and he came a little before ten, and fetched me away—my husband was then down in the cellar about his beer—Pratt and I went up the hill towards Sutton—Nye was in a fly at the top of the hill—we got into it, and drove off to London—we got to London very late—we could not find a place where there were separate rooms, and we all three passed the night in one room—next night we went to another house—Pratt and I passed
the night in the same bed there, and Nye had another room—I do not know where it was.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Was your husband a good husband to you? A. Yes, very good, and always very affectionate—I was not very much in love with Pratt—I cannot tell why I left my husband—Pratt has been wanting me to go away with him for these three or four months—I was not at all anxious to go away—I did not go till I was fetched—I gave Nye 8 s. to go and fetch my box—she asked me to pay for the box up to town—I gave her a sovereign before she went away, as she said she had no money—she packed the box up in a little store-room, and she took the things out of the drawers to put into it—a good many of them—I was not there half the time.
Q. Who told her where to find the things which were to go into the box? A. She had been up stairs before—she put them all out of my drawers—she asked me, and I told her she might do as she liked—they both told me to leave my husband penniless—they said so the same day that I went off—I was not very much shocked at it—I did not leave him penniless—when we got to town, we all three slept in one bed—I was dressed as I am now—I took my frock off, and my shawl and bonnet—I am twenty-nine years old—I have no family—I know that Pratt is married—I knew his wife—I had seen her once or twice—it might be more, but it was very little—I cannot tell How recently before I went off with him I had seen her—it might be four or five weeks, I cannot tell—I had not told her of the attention her husband was paying me—I have been entreated by her not to keep her husband out so much—she used to come to our house sometimes, but not very often—I had never been out with Pratt before this—I had been to Reigate with him—I do not know that I had been alone with him before this.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you tell Pratt, when your box was corded up, that your husband's things were inside it? A. They knew it—I told Nye that I was very uncomfortable about Pratt—she said she knew I had not been happy for a good while at home—I never gave Pratt any of my husband's liquor to drink—he and I used to drink together—sometimes he bought it, and sometimes my husband gave some, and we have drunk together when my husband has been out of the way—Pratt's wife only came there once—when my husband found me in the street, I had just left Pratt—Pratt had asked me to give him the money—I took five sovereigns out of the bag—the bandbox and umbrellas were in his possession—I did not leave them there—they were mine—Pratt had brought them up—they came up with us all—I did not bring them—I went out, and the bandbox and umbrellas were left on the table—he left them there—I did not, for I did not take them in my hand—he put them there, and as soon as Nye went away, he asked me for the money—I gave him six sovereigns—I took out five sovereigns for myself, and was doing the bag up—he asked me to give it to him, and I gave him the money, and went out to the top of the street—I do not know what I went out for—I did not ask him to take care of the money and things till I came in—I said, "Will you take care of the money till I come in?" but I did not say so of the tiling—I believe he said he would take care of them.
COURT. Q. Who packed up the money? A. I packed it up myself—I got it out of my husband's drawers in the bed-room—I was alone then—that was between six and seven o'clock—I put the bag into a drawer underneath
the other till I went away—the prisoners had left at three o'clock—I had not settled to go off with Pratt till the Monday, as this was on the Wednesday—he used to sit in the bar when he came to the house—that was the only room in which I used to meet him—he hardly ever used to be out of the bar—I did not walk with him—I have seen him in the street at Reigate, not in any house—I was once at a public-house at Reigate with him and his sister, not anywhere else.
JAMES ILLMAN re-examined. My wife is subject to fits, which affect her mind at times—I am a blacksmith by trade, which took me out a good deal, and the public-house was left to her—she had the last fit two or three months before she Jeff me—I cannot tell when before that—she has frequently had fits, I cannot say the time—Mr. Hampton, of Reigate, attended her for it—he did not attend her three months before she went away—he has attended her in illness from fits—he did not attend her at the fits, but she has been ill afterwards—he attended her some time in the beginning of the summer, I do not know what for, farther than for fits—she had had a fit just before—I have no doubt he knew that—I cannot tell when that was, I never set it down—I never knew her deranged, only from fits—she is subject to fits.
JAMES HENSLEY . I am in the service of Mr. Harrison, who keeps the Cock, at Sutton. On the 3rd of August I went with fly, and met Mrs. Illman and Pratt—they got into the fly I drove them to London, and put them down at the Bull, in Devonshire-street, Bishopsgate, between one and two o'clock in the morning—the house had closed.
WILLIAM COCKRELL ( police-constable L 108.) I was at the Elephant and Castle on the 5th of August—I saw Nye there in a cab, and a large box—I took her into custody, and took possession of the box—it contained the articles stated—I afterwards went to the West-end, and took Pratt into custody—I found on the table before him two umbrellas, a band-box containing a silk bonnet and several pieces of patchwork, and a basket containing a night-gown, a night-cap, a dirty sheet, and a gown—I asked him at the station what property he had about him—he said none but what belonged to him—I found some money on him—the only articles of male apparel in the box were two shirts and five silk handkerchiefs.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420822-2560]
2560. WILLIAM PRATT was again indicted for stealing, on the 5th of August, 1 bag, value 6 d.;55 sovereigns and 2 half-sovereigns; the property of James Illman.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES ILLMAN . I missed a bag containing 24 l. in gold, from a drawer up stairs—I cannot say How much more there was—I can only fix on that by my earnings.
ANN ILLMAN . I packed up sixty-one sovereigns, which I got from my husband's drawer—I took that with me when I went off" with the prisoner that night—he had before that asked me How much money I could take—I told him I did not know—he said I was to let him know before we went away; if I could take 100 l. I was to—I said I could not take 100 l.—he said I was to take as much as I could—the day before we went away I told him I had got 60 l. or 61 l.—he said that would do, if I could not take more, but if I could I was to, as he had none of his own—he paid for
the fly out of his own money—he had asked me to give him some money at the house, and I gave him 15 s. or 1 l. of my husband's money—I had some money besides the sixty-one sovereigns, loose in my pocket, and that I gave him to pay for the breakfast at the house where we slept—I afterwards gave him the bag containing fifty-five sovereigns and two half-sovereigns—that was at the public-house where my cousin put us on the Friday following—I took five sovereigns out, and gave him the remainder—he paid part of our expenses the day we went off, and on Friday—I do not know out of what money, without it was the 1 l. I had given him before we left the house—I do not know what money he had got of his own—there were two marked sovereigns in the bag I gave him—he said he would take me to Dover—I do not know what for.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you not take five sovereigns from the bag, give him the remainder, and ask him to take care of them till you came in? A. He had asked me several times before, and I said, "Will you take care of it, then, till I come back?"—he did not give me any answer—he took the money from my hand—it was not on the table—I asked him to take care of the money till I came in, and he said he would—he had asked me for it before, in the morning, I would not give it him, and before I came back I met my husband.
COURT. Q. He was not present when you packed up the money from your husband's drawer? A. No; but I had told him before, that I was going to take it—I had not committed any act of adultery with him before I went off with him—I put the money into my pocket when he fetched me away—he asked me for it before we got to town, I told him I had got it; and he asked me for it on the road, but I would not give it him—I do not know that I told him that night what I had got—I did not tell him where I had got it from—I had given him 1 l. at three o'clock, before we left the house—he asked me for it, to pay the expenses—I do not know what he paid on the road, nor How much for the fly, or the man that drove it—we had no refreshment on the road—he said he was glad he had got me away from my home—he has no family—he has been married twelve years, I believe—I hope I shall return to my husband from this Court—I do not know that I shall—I have not been with him since—I have not been in the same house with him; yes, he has slept in the same house, but in a different room, at my sister's—he has not promised to forgive me, if these prisoners are convicted.
WILLIAM COCKRILL ( police-constable L 108.) I took the prisoner into custody—I asked if he had any property about him—he said, none but what belonged to him—I then searched him, and found this bag of sovereigns wrapped up in a handkerchief in his breast-pocket—Illman, who was with me, immediately said, "That is my bag, I will swear to it"—the prisoner said, "How can you do that?"—he said, "I can"—"Well," he said, "if it is your bag, it is my money."
Cross-examined. Q. Did you ever state this before? A. Yes, at the police-court—I brought the wife away with the prisoner in a cab from the Duke of Wellington public-house.
COURT. Q. How did you get information where they were? A. In taking Nye to the station, she said, "Oh dear, what will become of me?"—I said, "I am sure I cannot tell you"—she said, "I never dreamt I should get into this bother; when my cousin sent for me this morning
to a public-house, "she said the wife and Pratt were stopping at a public-house.
JOSEPH KENT . I am a policeman. I was at Union Hall on the 5th of August when this matter was being inquired into—as Pratt was going out of the place we call "the slip," to be locked up, I heard him say to Mrs. Illman, "Mary Ann, say that the money and purse is mine"—she said she could not say so, for it was her husband's.
JAMES BAKER . I keep the Bull, in Devonshire-street, Bishopagate-street. I remember the prisoner coming to my house early in the morning, with Mrs. Illman and another woman—I had but one empty bed-room—I did not suppose they came to pass the night in that room, but they did so—next morning, about ten o'clock, as I was dressing, I heard some gold counted—I did not take any notice at the time, but after they were gone I thought there was something suspicious.
JAMES ILLMAN re-examined. This is my bag—I have had it ever since I have been married.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18420822-2561]
2561. WILLIAM POWER was indicted for embezzlement.
SAMUEL GILHAM . I am a baker, and live in Blackfriars-road. The prisoner was my journeyman—it was his duty to receive money for me, and pay me all he received—if he received 2 s. on the 12th of July, 2 s. on the 18th, and 2 s. on the 25th, he has not paid it to me.
SARAH DONE . I deal with Mr. Gilham—I paid the prisoner on the 12th of July 2 s., on the 18th, 2 s., and on the 25th 2 s., for his master.
Prisoner. It is my first offence; please to have mercy.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Four Months.

2564. DANIEL CHURCH and GEORGE BENTLIFF were indicted for stealing, on the 26th of July, 1 handkerchief, value 3 s. 6 d., the goods of Richard Gardner Cook, from his person.
RICHARD GARDNER COOK . I am a chemist, and live in High-street, Islington. On the 26th of July I was coming from the Surrey Zoological-gardens—after I had left a short time, there was a rush, and some one said, "You have lost your handkerchief"—I missed it, and Church, who was on the ground, was pointed out to me by Radcliffe—I went to the station, and my handkerchief was produced.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. There was a great crowd at that time? A. Yes, when we were coming out of the Gardens—the first thing I saw was Church on the ground, and Baker on him—I did not see Bentliff.
GEORGE BAKER . I am a carpenter. I was standing outside the Zoological-gardens—there were a great many people, and Radcliffe said there were three thieves going down—I then saw the prisoners following a gentleman, and Church took the handkerchief from the gentleman's pocket—Bentliff was behind Church, and he held the tails of his own coat up—I laid hold of Church, with the handkerchief in his hand, and we both fell down—I called to the gentleman that he had been robbed—I lost sight of Bentliff then, but I am sure be was with Church, and had the tails of his own coat up.
Cross-examined. Q. Has your name always been George Baker? A. Yes. I had been standing outside the gardens about three quarters of an hour—the company began to come out about half-past nine—I had been at work that day for my brother, in Nevill's-court—he is a printer's joiner—I was a policeman about six years, and discharged about eight months ago—I decline to say what I was discharged for—I never was here but once while I was a policeman, but I was a patrol before the police came on, and I attended here then a great many times—since I was in the police I have been here once, about a boy who stole a pin out of somebody's handkerchief—Radcliffe is an officer belonging to the Society for the Prevention of Cruelty—I met him that night by the Elephant and Castle—I had known him for some time—I had a job of work to go to next day—I was not looking out for a job.
Q. Did you receive 2 s. 6 d. when you were in the police? A. I was accused of it by a reputed thief—I did not receive it from him—the Commissioners did not make inquiries into it—I was charged before a magistrate with taking this 2 s. 6 d. to suppress evidence, the result was I was discharged from the police—the witness against me has been in prison since this, which I shall now be able to prove.
Q. When was it after the covering with the coat tails that you saw Bentliff again? A. On the 12th of the next month, when the Queen went to the house—I was then standing there by myself—Radcliffe was a witness with me in the case for stealing the pin.
THOMAS RADCLIFFE . I am an officer of the Animals' Friend Society. I was at the Zoological Gardens and noticed the two prisoners and another person—I spoke to Baker—I noticed Church following the gentleman, and the other two persons covering him, holding up his coat tails—I saw Church put his hand into the gentleman's pocket, but I could not see what he took out—Baker took hold of Church, and I took Bentliff, but seeing Baker and Church fall, I went to them.
Cross-examined. Q. What may you get for your friendship to animals? A. My pay is a guinea a week—I was at the Zoological Gardens on duty looking after the night cabs —I met Baker that night about half-past eight o'clock, near the Elephant and Castle—the people began to come out of the Gardens a little after nine—I have known Baker some time—I have been a witness with him once before—I will not swear How many times—he came to my house afterwards and said he had got one of the others, and I recognised Bentliff at Union-hall, the next day—I have heard that Baker had been turned out of the police for cheating
a man out of 2 s.6 d., but I know no harm in speaking to a man—when Baker and Church were on the ground I saw the handkerchief between their hands on the ground.
Church. I was knocked down, and Radcliffe fell over me with the handkerchief in his hand.
JURY. We have no confidence whatever in the evidence of these two witnesses.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18420822-2565]
2565. MARGARET DAVIS was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of July, 1 sovereign; 2 half-crowns; and 15 shillings; the monies of James Clark.
JAMES CLARK . I am a baker, and live in Park-place, Clapham. There was a sovereign, two half-crowns, and fifteen shillings, in a box on Sunday evening, the 27th of July.
SARAH CLARK . I am the prosecutor's wife. I saw the money safe in the box at my house at half-past two o'clock on the Monday—the prisoner lodged with us—she told me on the Monday she had no money, and on the Tuesday she left without notice, and at half-past nine that morning I missed the money—it was in this case—I know the purse now produced, it is the prisoner's.
CATHERINE CLARK . I am the prosecutor's daughter—I did not take any money out of the box—I heard the prisoner say she had no money to get any beer.
ELIZA STOFFELL . I am searcher at the station—I searched the prisoner on the Wednesday, she said she had no money about her—I found one half-crown in her hand, and two other half-crowns and three shillings in this purse.
Prisoner's Defence. Mrs. Clark gave me notice to quit as late as nine o'clock on Monday night; I said I would leave in the morning, which I did, she sent the officer after me, and said she had lost the money; I said as she had put me to such a nonplus I would not pay her my rent for a fortnight. There were other lodgers in the house; this money was what I worked at a laundresses for—I never said I had no money.
MRS. CLARK re-examined. She told me on Monday she had no money, and I gave her her supper—there were no other lodgers in the house—she only owed me one week's rent, and 1 s. I had lent her.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18420822-2566]
2566. CHARLES GIBB was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of August, 1 printed book, value 1 s.;1 cash-box, value 7 s.;2 half-sovereigns; 1 half-crown; 3 shillings; 6 sixpences; and 2 groats; the property of George Robson, his master; to which he pleaded
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18420822-2567]
2567. JAMES HERBERT was indicted for embezzlement.
EDWARD BURGESS . I am a cow-keeper, and live in Cornwall-road, Lambeth—the prisoner was in my employ, and it was his duty to pay me all he received—if lie received 13 s. from Alary Lofts he has not paid it to me.
Prisoner. I had 13 s. paid me, and I lost 1 s.; I gave the other to the policeman.
Witness. He gave it up to the policeman the next night, but when I asked if he had received this money, he said they had not paid it.
MARY LOFTS . I paid the prisoner 13 s., on the 27th of July, for the prosecutor.
THOMAS GOOSE ( police-constable L 160.) I took the prisoner—he said the female had not paid him—I said to the prosecutor, "It is a hard thing to take him up if he has not received it, if you will allow me I will take him to the person"—I took him to Lofts, and he denied it to her face—he said several times she had not paid him.
GUILTY .* Aged 16.— Transported for Seven Years.—Convict Ship.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18420822-2568]
2568. MARY ANN ALEXANDER was indicted for Stealing, on the 21st of July, 1 shift, value 10 s.;2 handkerchiefs, value 5 s.;1 tablecloth, value 10 s.;2 towels, value 2 s.6 d.;2 pairs of stockings, value 4 s.; and 1 habit shirt, value 10 s.; the goods of William Maughan, her master; and SARAH BOARDMAN for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen.
PAULINE MAUGHAN . I am the wife of William Maugban, and live in Bennondsey-street—Alexander was in my service till three weeks ago—I then discharged her, and on the Friday afterwards I missed some towels—I sent to Alexander, and she brought me back one towel—I afterwards missed two table-cloths, nineteen napkins, one dozen and seven cambric handkerchiefs, four habit-shirts, some stockings, some house cloths, dusters, four shifts, and a great many other things—I went the same day to Alexander, who was in Boardman's room—I asked her where the habit-shirts and other things were—at first she denied it—I said, "How ungrateful you have been to me, who have sent bread to your child"—she said, "You have been kind, but I hare taken nothing"—I was coming away, but she gave me this habit-shirt, and then gave me some daplicates from her pocket, and said, if I would forgive her she would tell me where all my other things were—I said that would not do—I would not promise to forgive her, as she had been so ungrateful—she then turned to Board man, and told her to give me the ticket of the table-clath—Boardman said, she had no such thing—'Alexander said, "Yes, you know you have"—Boardman then said they had sold the table-doth—I afterwards got a policeman and went with him to their room again—we found them both sitting together by the fire—they bad cut the tops of my stockings off, and put them on the fire, and the feet and legs of the stockings were on the floor—we found two of my handkerchiefs, which they were burning—the officer took possession of them—here are the tops of the stockings, and the mark on them, and here are the legs and feet of them.
RICHARD FULLER ( police-constable M 120.) Mrs. Maughan took me to a house in Bermondsey, where I saw the prisoners sitting in chairs—they were not doing anything—the prosecutrix said something was burning—she went to the fire—I saw her pull this handkerchief out, and these tops of stockings—I put the fire out with my feet, and picked up these other tops of stockings—they have marks on them—I received these two duplicates from Mrs. Maughan before I went to the house—this table-cloth was given up to me at Union Hall, by Larkman.
ANN LARKMAN . I am the wife of William Larkman; I live in Bermondsey. I bought this table-cloth of Boardman for 2 s., in July.
ROBERT PLUSH . I am shopman to Mr. Grose, a pawnbroker. I produce a shift, two towels, a duster, and a cloth, pawned at different times by a woman, in the name of Boardman—I cannot say by whom—these are two of the duplicates I gave to the person who pawned the things.
MRS. MAUGHAN. These two duplicates were given me by Alexander.
Boardman. I received nothing from Alexander but the table-cloth; she brought the things to my house, and said the lady had given them to her—I never pledged any thing.
ALEXANDER — GUILTY . Aged 22.
BOARDMAN — GUILTY . Aged 40.
Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Baron Rolfe.
[trialAccount t18420822-2569]
2569. GEORGE EDWARDS was indicted for feloniously uttering counterfeit coin, he having been before convicted of a like offence.
MR. CHARNOCK conducted the Prosecution.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL . I am assistant-solicitor to the Royal Mint I produce a copy of the record of the conviction of George Edmonds at this Court, in the January Session, 1838—I have examined it with the original record—it is correct—( read.)
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to the Royal Mint—I was present at the trial of George Edmonds—the prisoner is the person.
MARY ANN BINKS . I am the wife of Litchfield Binks, of the Musical Repository, Frederick-place, Southwark. On the 10th of July, between three and five o'clock in the afternoon) the prisoner came and asked for two boxes of resin—he paid me a five-shilling piece—I gave him a half-crown, a shilling, and a sixpence in change—he went away—I had put the crown-piece into my pocket—I had no other crown-piece there—in about three minutes he returned—he said had I not made a mistake—was I aware what money I had given him—I said, "Yes, four shillings"—he said his father never paid more than 3 d. a box for the resin—I said, "If your father is in the profession he shall have them for 4 d. each," and I returned him the 4 d.—he said if his father did not like to have them at that price, would I return the money, and take the resin back—I said yes, and he left the shop—I am sure he is the person—I never parted with the crown-piece till I gave it to West.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What did you do with it after you put it into your pocket? A. I took it out, and put it on the piano-forte while the prisoner was there—I did not lose sight of it in case he should want it back—I did not put any mark on it till the following day—I was examined twice—on the first occasion I said I believed he was the person—he was differently dressed, and I said I could tell more by the general appearance and the speech than the dress—he was not with me more than three minutes.
JOHN WEST ( police-constable M 242.) I produce a crown-piece, which I received from Binks.
WILLIAM JOHN MARTIN PENNEY . I am the son of Joseph Penney, a publican, keeping the Indian Arms, at Rotherhithe. On Friday, the 22nd of July, the prisoner came to the bar, and called for a glass of half-and-half
—I drew it him, and he gave me a shilling—I saw it was bad, and took it to my father.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you quite sure he is the man? A. Yes, I had never seen him before—he was in the place a few minutes—when I went out he was standing before the next public-house—I followed him up—he went op the Globe-stairs—I gave him to the policeman—I did not see him all the way to the Globe-stairs—I lost sight of him while I was telling the policeman.
JOSEPH PENNET . I am the father of William Penney. On the 22nd of July he brought me a bad shilling—the prisoner was at the bar—I went, and asked if he was aware of what he had given the boy—he said I was to take for a glass of half-and-half—I said I had a good mind to take him, and asked if he was aware that the shilling was bad—he said he was not—he then tendered me a good sixpence to take for the glass of half-and-half—I gave him 4 1/2 d. change—he then held his hand for the return of the shilling—he asked me to allow him to look at it—I said I would not give it out of my hand—he said he would see whether I would return the shilling or not, and went out of the house—I then sent my son up the street to give information to the policeman—I put the shilling on a shelf in the bar—no one else was present when I put it there—I gave it to the policeman.
Cross-examined. Q. What did you do when you put it on the shelf? A. I went about my own business—I did not mark it before I put it there—it was about an hour before the policeman came—there was no other shilling there, and the bar is quite private—the shelf was over my head—I could not see what was on it—I cannot swear whether what I said was, "Is that the shilling you gave my son?"—I said it was a bad one, and he was aware of it—I had never seen him before—the Globestairs is half a mile from our house—my son went out directly the prisoner left—the prisoner was not out of my sight as far as I could see up the street.
THOMAS TOWERSAY ( police-constable M 237.) I received this shilling from Mr. Penney—I searched the prisoner, and found on him three keys—I gave them to the sergeant in the office.
JOHN JULIAN ( police-sergeant M 26.) I received some keys from Towersay—I kept them in my possession till we went before the Magistrate, and then gave them to Millis, by the Magistrate's direction.
THOMAS MILLIS ( police-constable M 222.) I received some keys from Julian—I went to the prisoner's lodging, in Lock's-square, Walworth, with his brother—I tried first the street-door key—it opened the lock—I tried a room in the house, which one of the other keys fitted—I searched the room, and found there a ladle, a tobacco-pipe, four files, a can of sand, and four spoons of Britannia metal.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it the prisoner who asked for the keys? A. No.
THOMAS SHOWELL . I live in Lock's-square, Walworth—the prisoner lodged in my house five or six weeks—I was at home when Millis came there—I pointed out to him the room the prisoner occupied.
Cross-examined. Q. Did anybody else live in the room? A. No.
MR. JOHN FIELD re-examined. These are both counterfeit.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Transported for Fourteen Years.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

2571. CHARLES BROWN was indicted for a misdemeanor.
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined One Year.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18420822-2572]
2572. JOSEPH WOOD was indicted for a misdemeanor.
HENRY JONES . I am foreman to Mr. James Child, a market gardener, at Putney. On the 13th of August the prisoner came to me, and asked me to let him have some savoy cabbage-plants for Mr. Winks—I know Mr. Winks—I told the prisoner at first that he could not have them—he said he did not know what he should do, he should not be paid for his time—I told him at last he could have them—he went to my master's place and got them—I saw him pull part of them—he took away 100—I let him have them, because he came and said he wanted them for Mr. Winks—I put them down to Mr. Winks' account.
Prisoner. I had been planting, and I misplanted some—I then went and got this 100 without orders.
THOMAS WINKS . I live near the Royal Hospital, at Chelsea. The prisoner was in my service, but not at the time he went for these plants—I did not send him for any savoy cabbage-plants then—he had been sent for some before, when he was at work for me.
WILLIAM EDWIN . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner.
GUILTY . Aged 50.— Confined Nine Days.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, SEPTEMBER 19 TH, 1842.
